It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. I completely refuse to get behind the wheel, and I change the subject immediately if anyone mentions road trips or turns on music from that festival weekend. If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?"
  }

It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. It's been six days now, and they haven't really let up or changed in intensity at all.

If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. It's been six days now, and they haven't really let up or changed in intensity at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the symptoms have been constant since the accident six days ago, but can you tell me if you've noticed any slight variations in how often the memories or flashbacks occur, or if they seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: The memories feel pretty constant, but they definitely hit me hardest when I'm trying to quiet my mind, like when I'm grading papers or just making coffee. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway."
  }

They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?"

Although she and the other driver survived with only minor physical injuries, she spent several terrifying minutes trapped in the vehicle, smelling gasoline and listening to the other driver scream until first responders arrived and cut her out. Over the next week, her normally grounding routines—her Red River walking club, her art-therapy volunteering, even planning classes at the university—became increasingly disrupted as she felt unable to get back behind the wheel to commute and found herself breaking down when colleagues mentioned road trips or asked about her weekend. After six days of struggling to get to campus, canceling a book club meeting, and relying on her husband to drive her everywhere, she went to the university counseling center at the urging of a fellow faculty member, worried that her reactions to the crash were \"taking over\" her life.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): All week it’s like my brain keeps hitting replay on those few minutes after the crash—hearing the other driver screaming, smelling the gasoline, seeing the windshield spiderweb—and it pops into my head full force when I’m trying to grade papers or even just make coffee, and I feel my whole body clamp up like I’m back in the car again.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve been going out of my way to steer clear of anything that might remind me of it—refusing to drive, changing the subject if someone mentions road trips, turning off songs that were playing at the festival—and if my husband even suggests taking the highway, I just shut down and insist we stay home.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a picture she drew, I just feel flat and disconnected, like I’m watching someone else’s life and can’t access that usual sense of joy or warmth I know I’m supposed to feel.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I keep snapping at my husband and my students over little things—like him asking if I’m okay one more time or a student emailing late—and I hear myself barking back or using a sharp tone, but it’s like the anger comes out of nowhere before I can catch it.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Since the accident, every sudden noise—a door slamming in the hallway, a car horn outside, even a dropped book in the classroom—makes me jump out of my skin, my heart races, and it takes several minutes for me to stop shaking and feel like I’m actually in the room again.\n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): Several times a day I get this weird, floaty feeling where the campus hallways and even my own living room look kind of far away and unreal, and I feel like I’m watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the days since the crash, I’ve had to cancel my book club, skip my walking group, and cut back on time at the university because I can’t bring myself to drive or stay focused long enough to teach or plan classes the way I normally do.\n- duration: These reactions have been happening nonstop since the car accident last week and haven’t really let up over the past six days.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(No match in profile or inference only)\nAnswer strategy: State that you haven't really noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight since the accident, keeping the response brief and focused on the fact that this hasn't been an issue."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off. It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. I completely refuse to get behind the wheel, and I change the subject immediately if anyone mentions road trips or turns on music from that festival weekend. If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. It's been six days now, and they haven't really let up or changed in intensity at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the symptoms have been constant since the accident six days ago, but can you tell me if you've noticed any slight variations in how often the memories or flashbacks occur, or if they seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: The memories feel pretty constant, but they definitely hit me hardest when I'm trying to quiet my mind, like when I'm grading papers or just making coffee. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?"
  }

No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off. It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. I completely refuse to get behind the wheel, and I change the subject immediately if anyone mentions road trips or turns on music from that festival weekend. If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'floaty feeling' like you're watching yourself from over your shoulder at times, can you tell me more about when this happens and how often you experience it?"

Explain that several times a day, your surroundings (like campus hallways or your living room) suddenly look far away and unreal, and you feel like you are watching yourself from over your shoulder rather than being in your own body. Clarify that while it can happen when you are stressed by memories, it also seems to happen out of nowhere during your normal day-to-day routine."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off. It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. I completely refuse to get behind the wheel, and I change the subject immediately if anyone mentions road trips or turns on music from that festival weekend. If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. It's been six days now, and they haven't really let up or changed in intensity at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the symptoms have been constant since the accident six days ago, but can you tell me if you've noticed any slight variations in how often the memories or flashbacks occur, or if they seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: The memories feel pretty constant, but they definitely hit me hardest when I'm trying to quiet my mind, like when I'm grading papers or just making coffee. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'floaty feeling' like you're watching yourself from over your shoulder at times, can you tell me more about when this happens and how often you experience it?\n\nPatient: It happens several times a day, and during those moments, the campus hallways or even my own living room look really far away and unreal. It's this strange, floaty feeling where I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by this 'floaty feeling' and whether it's related to the memories of the accident or if it happens independently of those memories?"

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off. It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. I completely refuse to get behind the wheel, and I change the subject immediately if anyone mentions road trips or turns on music from that festival weekend. If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. It's been six days now, and they haven't really let up or changed in intensity at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the symptoms have been constant since the accident six days ago, but can you tell me if you've noticed any slight variations in how often the memories or flashbacks occur, or if they seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: The memories feel pretty constant, but they definitely hit me hardest when I'm trying to quiet my mind, like when I'm grading papers or just making coffee. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'floaty feeling' like you're watching yourself from over your shoulder at times, can you tell me more about when this happens and how often you experience it?\n\nPatient: It happens several times a day, and during those moments, the campus hallways or even my own living room look really far away and unreal. It's this strange, floaty feeling where I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by this 'floaty feeling' and whether it's related to the memories of the accident or if it happens independently of those memories?\n\nPatient: It's a feeling where my surroundings, like the campus hallways or my own living room, suddenly look really far away and completely unreal. I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body. While it can happen when I'm stressed by the memories, it also seems to happen completely out of nowhere during my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is something that's been present before the car accident, or if it started after the accident and seems to be directly related to the traumatic experience?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'floaty feeling' like you're watching yourself from over your shoulder at times, can you tell me more about when this happens and how often you experience it?\n\nPatient: It happens several times a day, and during those moments, the campus hallways or even my own living room look really far away and unreal. It's this strange, floaty feeling where I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by this 'floaty feeling' and whether it's related to the memories of the accident or if it happens independently of those memories?\n\nPatient: It's a feeling where my surroundings, like the campus hallways or my own living room, suddenly look really far away and completely unreal. I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body. While it can happen when I'm stressed by the memories, it also seems to happen completely out of nowhere during my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is something that's been present before the car accident, or if it started after the accident and seems to be directly related to the traumatic experience?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before the accident; it is completely new and only started after the crash last week. Since then, it’s been happening several times a day, making my surroundings look far away and unreal while I feel like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is happening at the same time as the flashbacks of the accident, or are these two separate experiences for you?"

Although she and the other driver survived with only minor physical injuries, she spent several terrifying minutes trapped in the vehicle, smelling gasoline and listening to the other driver scream until first responders arrived and cut her out. Over the next week, her normally grounding routines—her Red River walking club, her art-therapy volunteering, even planning classes at the university—became increasingly disrupted as she felt unable to get back behind the wheel to commute and found herself breaking down when colleagues mentioned road trips or asked about her weekend. After six days of struggling to get to campus, canceling a book club meeting, and relying on her husband to drive her everywhere, she went to the university counseling center at the urging of a fellow faculty member, worried that her reactions to the crash were \"taking over\" her life.\n- Chief Complaint: “Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S069 (Intrusive_Symptoms): All week it’s like my brain keeps hitting replay on those few minutes after the crash—hearing the other driver screaming, smelling the gasoline, seeing the windshield spiderweb—and it pops into my head full force when I’m trying to grade papers or even just make coffee, and I feel my whole body clamp up like I’m back in the car again.\n- S070 (Trauma_Avoidance): I’ve been going out of my way to steer clear of anything that might remind me of it—refusing to drive, changing the subject if someone mentions road trips, turning off songs that were playing at the festival—and if my husband even suggests taking the highway, I just shut down and insist we stay home.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a picture she drew, I just feel flat and disconnected, like I’m watching someone else’s life and can’t access that usual sense of joy or warmth I know I’m supposed to feel.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I keep snapping at my husband and my students over little things—like him asking if I’m okay one more time or a student emailing late—and I hear myself barking back or using a sharp tone, but it’s like the anger comes out of nowhere before I can catch it.\n- S080 (Exaggerated_Startle_Response): Since the accident, every sudden noise—a door slamming in the hallway, a car horn outside, even a dropped book in the classroom—makes me jump out of my skin, my heart races, and it takes several minutes for me to stop shaking and feel like I’m actually in the room again.\n- S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization): Several times a day I get this weird, floaty feeling where the campus hallways and even my own living room look kind of far away and unreal, and I feel like I’m watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the days since the crash, I’ve had to cancel my book club, skip my walking group, and cut back on time at the university because I can’t bring myself to drive or stay focused long enough to teach or plan classes the way I normally do.\n- duration: These reactions have been happening nonstop since the car accident last week and haven’t really let up over the past six days.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S082 (Depersonalization_Derealization), Chief Complaint\nAnswer strategy: Confirm that this 'floaty' feeling is highly unsettling and distressing, rather than comforting or neutral. Describe it as a weird, scary experience that makes you feel disconnected from reality and contributes to your worry that these reactions are taking over your life."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off. It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. I completely refuse to get behind the wheel, and I change the subject immediately if anyone mentions road trips or turns on music from that festival weekend. If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. It's been six days now, and they haven't really let up or changed in intensity at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the symptoms have been constant since the accident six days ago, but can you tell me if you've noticed any slight variations in how often the memories or flashbacks occur, or if they seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: The memories feel pretty constant, but they definitely hit me hardest when I'm trying to quiet my mind, like when I'm grading papers or just making coffee. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'floaty feeling' like you're watching yourself from over your shoulder at times, can you tell me more about when this happens and how often you experience it?\n\nPatient: It happens several times a day, and during those moments, the campus hallways or even my own living room look really far away and unreal. It's this strange, floaty feeling where I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by this 'floaty feeling' and whether it's related to the memories of the accident or if it happens independently of those memories?\n\nPatient: It's a feeling where my surroundings, like the campus hallways or my own living room, suddenly look really far away and completely unreal. I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body. While it can happen when I'm stressed by the memories, it also seems to happen completely out of nowhere during my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is something that's been present before the car accident, or if it started after the accident and seems to be directly related to the traumatic experience?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before the accident; it is completely new and only started after the crash last week. Since then, it’s been happening several times a day, making my surroundings look far away and unreal while I feel like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is happening at the same time as the flashbacks of the accident, or are these two separate experiences for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they happen at the exact same time. To me, they really feel like two separate, distinct experiences when they occur.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is distressing to you, or if it's more of a neutral or even comforting experience?"
  }

It is definitely not comforting; in fact, it’s incredibly unsettling and scary to feel so disconnected from reality. Experiencing that several times a day is highly distressing and just makes me worry even more that these reactions are completely taking over my life.

Although she and the other driver survived with only minor physical injuries, she spent several terrifying minutes trapped in the vehicle, smelling gasoline and listening to the other driver scream until first responders arrived and cut her out. Over the next week, her normally grounding routines—her Red River walking club, her art-therapy volunteering, even planning classes at the university—became increasingly disrupted as she felt unable to get back behind the wheel to commute and found herself breaking down when colleagues mentioned road trips or asked about her weekend. After six days of struggling to get to campus, canceling a book club meeting, and relying on her husband to drive her everywhere, she went to the university counseling center at the urging of a fellow     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the car accident last week, I keep reliving it over and over in my head, even when I’m trying to work or relax, and I can’t seem to shut it off. It feels like my brain is just constantly stuck on what happened and I'm really struggling to function.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the car accident, or is it just the memories that are bothering you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm definitely avoiding anything related to it. I completely refuse to get behind the wheel, and I change the subject immediately if anyone mentions road trips or turns on music from that festival weekend. If my husband even suggests taking the highway, I completely shut down and insist we just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any difficulty sleeping or an exaggerated startle response since the accident, such as being easily startled by loud noises?\n\nPatient: Sleep hasn't really been the main issue for me, but I am incredibly jumpy now. Any sudden noise, like a door slamming or a car horn, makes me jump out of my skin and my heart race. It takes me several minutes to stop shaking and feel like I'm actually back in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having difficulty concentrating on tasks or feeling constantly 'on guard' since the accident, like you're waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm having a really hard time staying focused long enough to teach or plan my classes because memories of the crash keep popping into my head while I'm grading papers or even making coffee. I also feel constantly on guard, jumping out of my skin at sudden noises like a door slamming or a car horn, which leaves me shaking with my heart racing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling detached from others or having trouble experiencing positive emotions, such as happiness or love, since the car accident?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been feeling completely flat and disconnected from everyone. Even when my walking club texts me silly photos or my niece sends me a drawing, I can't seem to access my usual warmth or joy. It honestly feels like I'm just watching someone else's life from the outside.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking about what you could have done differently to prevent the car accident, or feeling guilty about something related to the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling guilty or thinking about what I could have done differently, because the truck ran the light so fast there was no way to prevent it. My mind is just too overwhelmed by the aftermath of the crash to even focus on things like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these symptoms, and have they been constant since the car accident or have they changed in intensity over time?\n\nPatient: These reactions have been happening nonstop ever since the car accident last week. It's been six days now, and they haven't really let up or changed in intensity at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the symptoms have been constant since the accident six days ago, but can you tell me if you've noticed any slight variations in how often the memories or flashbacks occur, or if they seem to be triggered by anything in particular?\n\nPatient: The memories feel pretty constant, but they definitely hit me hardest when I'm trying to quiet my mind, like when I'm grading papers or just making coffee. They also get triggered instantly by specific reminders, like someone mentioning a road trip, hearing songs that were playing at the festival, or any talk of driving on the highway.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms started right after the accident and have been constant, but can you tell me more about what happens when you're not actively reminded of the accident, such as during quiet moments or when engaged in activities you enjoy, do the flashbacks or memories still occur as frequently?\n\nPatient: Yes, even during quiet moments when I'm just trying to relax, my brain suddenly hits replay on the crash without any warning. While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'floaty feeling' like you're watching yourself from over your shoulder at times, can you tell me more about when this happens and how often you experience it?\n\nPatient: It happens several times a day, and during those moments, the campus hallways or even my own living room look really far away and unreal. It's this strange, floaty feeling where I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by this 'floaty feeling' and whether it's related to the memories of the accident or if it happens independently of those memories?\n\nPatient: It's a feeling where my surroundings, like the campus hallways or my own living room, suddenly look really far away and completely unreal. I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body. While it can happen when I'm stressed by the memories, it also seems to happen completely out of nowhere during my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is something that's been present before the car accident, or if it started after the accident and seems to be directly related to the traumatic experience?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before the accident; it is completely new and only started after the crash last week. Since then, it’s been happening several times a day, making my surroundings look far away and unreal while I feel like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is happening at the same time as the flashbacks of the accident, or are these two separate experiences for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they happen at the exact same time. To me, they really feel like two separate, distinct experiences when they occur.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is distressing to you, or if it's more of a neutral or even comforting experience?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not comforting; in fact, it’s incredibly unsettling and scary to feel so disconnected from reality. Experiencing that several times a day is highly distressing and just makes me worry even more that these reactions are completely taking over my life.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often the 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body happens in relation to the flashbacks, and if it's possible for you to recall any specific details or sensations during those episodes?"
  }

During those moments, my surroundings like the campus hallways or my living room look completely far away and unreal, and I feel the strange sensation of watching myself talk and move from over my shoulder.

While doing mundane tasks like making coffee or grading, I'll suddenly smell the gasoline and hear the other driver screaming, and my whole body tenses up as if I'm trapped back in the car again.\n\nDoctor: When you're experiencing these flashbacks or memories, do you feel like you're reliving the accident as if it's happening all over again, or do you feel more like you're watching it happen from outside your body?\n\nPatient: When the memories hit, it feels intensely real, like my body is clamping up and I'm actually back in the car re-experiencing the smells and sounds. I do get a strange, floaty feeling like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder, but that happens at other times during the day, like when I'm walking down campus hallways, rather than during the actual flashbacks.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've experienced any other symptoms such as significant weight loss or gain, or changes in your appetite since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in my appetite or weight since the accident. That side of things hasn't really been an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned a 'floaty feeling' like you're watching yourself from over your shoulder at times, can you tell me more about when this happens and how often you experience it?\n\nPatient: It happens several times a day, and during those moments, the campus hallways or even my own living room look really far away and unreal. It's this strange, floaty feeling where I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe in more detail what you mean by this 'floaty feeling' and whether it's related to the memories of the accident or if it happens independently of those memories?\n\nPatient: It's a feeling where my surroundings, like the campus hallways or my own living room, suddenly look really far away and completely unreal. I feel like I'm watching myself move and talk from just over my shoulder instead of actually being in my own body. While it can happen when I'm stressed by the memories, it also seems to happen completely out of nowhere during my normal day-to-day routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is something that's been present before the car accident, or if it started after the accident and seems to be directly related to the traumatic experience?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before the accident; it is completely new and only started after the crash last week. Since then, it’s been happening several times a day, making my surroundings look far away and unreal while I feel like I'm watching myself from over my shoulder.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if this 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is happening at the same time as the flashbacks of the accident, or are these two separate experiences for you?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they happen at the exact same time. To me, they really feel like two separate, distinct experiences when they occur.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if the 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body is distressing to you, or if it's more of a neutral or even comforting experience?\n\nPatient: It is definitely not comforting; in fact, it’s incredibly unsettling and scary to feel so disconnected from reality. Experiencing that several times a day is highly distressing and just makes me worry even more that these reactions are completely taking over my life.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how often the 'floaty feeling' of watching yourself from outside your body happens in relation to the flashbacks, and if it's possible for you to recall any specific details or sensations during those episodes?\n\nPatient: The floaty feeling happens several times a day, often kicking in when I get overwhelmed by those intrusive memories of the screaming, the gasoline smell, and the spiderweb windshield as a way to disconnect. During those moments, my surroundings like the campus hallways or my living room look completely far away and unreal, and I feel the strange sensation of watching myself talk and move from over my shoulder."

Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
